is arbitrary. Hence indeed
\varphi+_{r}\psi\leq\omega+_{r}\psi
.

Let
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathrm{A}}
be a directed set in
\mathbf{vN}(M,N)
with supremum
\varphi
. For each
h\in H
, we have

\|(r\varphi_{\alpha}+(1-r)\psi)(x)h-(r\varphi+(1-r)\psi)(x)h\|=\|r\varphi_{\alpha}(x)h-r\varphi(x)h\|=|r|\|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)h-\varphi(x)h\|,

which clearly converges to
0
, hence
\bigvee_{\alpha\in\mathrm{A}}(r\varphi_{\alpha}(x)+(1-r)\psi(x)=r\varphi(x)+(1-r)\psi(x)
, whence
\bigvee_{\alpha\in\mathrm{A}}(\varphi_{\alpha}+_{r}\psi)=\varphi+_{r}\psi
. We conclude that
+_{r}:\mathbf{vN}(M,N)\times\mathbf{vN}(M,N)\to\mathbf{vN}(M,N)
is Scott continuous in the first variable, and since
\varphi+_{r}\psi=\psi+_{1-r}\varphi
, it also follows that
+_{r}
is Scott continuous in the second variable, hence Scott continuous overall.

Since
r\cdot\varphi=\varphi+_{r}0
, it follows that scalar multiplication is Scott continuous in
\varphi
, so we only have to check that it is Scott continuous in
r
. So fix
\varphi\in\mathbf{vN}(M,N)
, and let
D\subseteq[0,1]
be a directed set with supremum
s
.
We need to show that
\bigvee_{d\in D}d\varphi=s\varphi
, hence for each
x\in M
, we need to show that
(d\varphi(x))_{d\in D}
converges to
s\varphi(x)
in the strong operator topology. Thus we need to show for each
x\in M
and each
h\in H
that

\|d\varphi(x)h-s\varphi(x)h\|
converges to zero. But
\|d\varphi(x)-s\varphi(x)\|=|d-s|\|\varphi(x)\|
and obviously
|d-s|
converges to
0
since
s
is the supremum of
D
in
[0,1]
, which show that scalar multiplication is also Scott continuous in both variables, hence Scott continuous overall.
∎

Theorem A.0.9
.

The category
\mathbf{vN}
is enriched over
\mathbf{KS}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.0.8
, any homset in
\mathbf{vN}
is a Kegelspitze. We have to verify that for any von Neumann algebras
M
,
N
, and
R
, and any normal completely positive subunital map
\varphi:M\to N
, the maps

\mathbf{vN}(R,\varphi):\mathbf{vN}(R,M)\to\mathbf{vN}(R,N),\quad\psi\mapsto\varphi\circ\psi

and

\mathbf{vN}(\varphi,R):\mathbf{vN}(N,R)\to\mathbf{vN}(M,R),\quad\psi\mapsto\psi\circ\varphi

are morphisms in
\mathbf{KS}
. It follows from
(
Cho 2016
, Theorem 5.3)
that
\mathbf{vN}
is enriched over
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\perp!}
, hence the morphisms above are Scott continuous. By Lemma
A.0.7
, the maps are linear, hence indeed morphisms in
\mathbf{KS}
. ∎

Appendix B
The isomorphism
r_{X}

Definition B.0.1.

Let
\mathcal{L}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{DCPO}
be the functor defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}X

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}(X,\sqsubseteq),\text{ where }\sqsubseteq\text{ is the discrete order on $X$}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}f

Definition B.0.2.

A complex Banach algebra
A
is called a
*
-algebra
if it equipped with an idempotent conjugate-linear map
*:A\to A
such that
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
for each
a,b\in A
. If, in addition,
\|a^{*}a\|=\|a\|^{2}
for each
a\in A
, we call
A
a
C*-algebra
.

In particular any von Neumann algebra is a C*-algebra. More generally, for each Hilbert space
H
, every norm-closed
*
-subalgebra of
B(H)
is a C*-algebra. The converse holds as well: if
A
is a C*-algebra, any
*
-homomorphism
\pi:A\to B(H)
for some Hilbert space
H
is called a
representation
. The Gelfand-Naimark Representation Theorem
(
Blackadar 2006
, Corollary II.6.4.10)
states that any C*-algebra has a faithful representation.

Definition B.0.3.

Any C*-algebra that is
*
-isomorphic to a von Neumann algebra is called a
W*-algebra
.

Let
V
be a Banach space. The space of all continuous linear maps
V\to\mathbb{C}
is denoted by
V^{*}
.

The following theorem gives an alternative characterization of W*-algebras:

Theorem B.0.4
.

(
Blackadar 2006
, III.2.1.8, Theorems III.2.4.1 & III.2.4.2)

Let
A
be a C*-algebra. Then
A
is a W*-algebra if and only if there is a Banach space
F
such that
A
is isometrically isomorphic to the dual
F^{*}
of
F
. Under the isometric embedding of
F
into
F^{**}
, and by the isometric isomorphism
A^{*}\cong F^{**}
,
F
is isometrically isomorphic to
A_{*}
, the subspace of
A^{*}
consisting of all normal functionals on
A
.

Hence if
M
is a W*-algebra, then up to isometric isomorphism
M_{*}
is the unique Banach space whose dual is isometrically isomorphic to
M
. We call
M_{*}
the
predual
of
M
.

Let
X
be a set. From Example
4.1.3
we already know that
\ell^{\infty}(X)
is a von Neumann algebra. Its predual is the the Banach space of all functions
f:X\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\sum_{x\in X}|f(x)|<\infty
, which we denote by
\ell^{1}(X)
, see for instance Table B.1, p. 547 of
(
Landsman 2017
)
. By the same table, we have a
separating
pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:\ell^{1}(X)\times\ell^{\infty}(X)\to\mathbb{C}
,
(f,g)\mapsto\sum_{x\in X}f(x)g(x)
, i.e., for each different
f,f^{\prime}\in\ell^{1}(X)
there exists a
g\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
such that
\langle f,g\rangle\neq\langle f^{\prime},g\rangle
, and for each different
g,g^{\prime}\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
there is an
f\in\ell^{1}(X)
such that
\langle f,g\rangle\neq\langle f,g^{\prime}\rangle
. Using
(
Landsman 2017
, Theorem B.47)
, this pairing induces an isomorphism of vector spaces
\ell^{1}(X)\to\ell^{\infty}(X)_{*}
, which is isometric as one can see as follows. Let
g\in\ell^{1}(X)
, so
\|g\|=\sum_{x\in X}|g(x)|<\infty
. Let
f\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
such that
\|f\|=1
, i.e.,
\sup_{x\in X}|f(x)|=1
.
Then

\|g\|=\|f\|\|g\|=\sup_{x\in X}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X}|f(x)||g(x^{\prime})|\geq\sum_{x\in X}|f(x)||g(x)|\geq\left|\sum_{x\in X}f(x)g(x)\right|=|\zeta(g)(f)|,

hence

\|\zeta(g)\|=\sup\{|\zeta(g)(f)|:f\in\ell^{\infty}(X),\|f\|=1\}\leq\|g\|
. On the other hand, for ach
x\in X
, we can write
g(x)=|g(x)|e^{i\arg(g(x))}
. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{C}
be given by
f(x)=e^{-i\arg(g(x))}
. Then
|f(x)|=1
for each
x\in X
, hence
f\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
with
\|f\|=1
. Then we have

\|\zeta(g)\|\geq|\zeta(g)(f)|=\left|\sum_{x\in X}f(x)g(x)\right|=\left|\sum_{x\in X}|g(x)|\right|=\|g\|,

so
\|\zeta(g)\|=\|g\|
, showing that
\zeta
is an isometry. We conclude:

Lemma B.0.5
.

We have an isometric isomorphism
\zeta:\ell^{1}(X)\to\ell^{\infty}(X)_{*}
defined by

\zeta(g)(f)=\sum_{x\in X}f(x)g(x)

for each
g\in\ell^{1}(X)
and each
f\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}(X)
be the set of all
g\in\ell^{1}(X)
such that
g(x)\geq 0
for each
x\in X
and
\sum_{x\in X}g(x)\leq 1
. We order
\mathcal{D}(X)
by
g\leq g^{\prime}
if and only if
g(x)\leq g^{\prime}(x)
for each
x\in X
.

Proposition B.0.6
.

The isometric isomorphism
\zeta:\ell^{1}(X)\to\ell^{\infty}(X)_{*}
restricts to an isomorphism of Kegelspitzen
\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathbf{vN}(\ell^{\infty}(X),\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\ell^{1}(X)
. Then
\zeta(g)\in\ell^{\infty}(X)^{*}
is positive if and only if
\zeta(g)(f)
is positive for each positive
f\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
, i.e.,
\sum_{x\in X}f(x)g(x)\geq 0
for each positive
f\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
. Since
f
is positive if and only if
f(x)\geq 0
for each
x\in x
, and for each
y\in X
the function
e_{y}:X\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
e_{y}(x)=\delta_{x,y}
for each
x\in X
is an element of
\ell^{\infty}(X)
that clearly is positive, it follows that
\zeta(g)(f)
is positive if and only if
g(x)\geq 0
for each
x\in X
. It also follows that if
g^{\prime}\in\ell^{1}(X)
is another element such that
\zeta(g^{\prime})
is positive, then
\zeta(g)\leq\zeta(g^{\prime})
if and only if
\zeta(g^{\prime}-g)
is positive if and only if
g(x)\leq g^{\prime}(x)
for each
x\in X
.

By
(
Blackadar 2006
, Proposition II.6.2.5)
, any positive
\omega\in\ell^{\infty}(X)^{*}
is bounded with norm
\|\omega\|=\omega(1)
. Thus
\omega
is subunital if and only if
\|\omega\|\leq 1
. Hence, if
\zeta(g)
positive, it is bounded with norm
\|\zeta(g)\|=\zeta(g)(1)
, so
\|\zeta(g)\|=\sum_{x\in X}g(x)
. Thus
\zeta(g)
is positive and subunital if and only if
\|g\|=\|\zeta(g)\|=\sum_{x\in X}g(x)\leq 1
, where we used that
\zeta
is an isometry. It follows that
\zeta
restricts to an isometric isomorphism between
\mathbf{vN}(\ell^{\infty}(X),\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{D}(X)
, which is also an order isomorphism. Since
\zeta
is an isomorphisms of vector spaces, it in particular preserve the convex structure, whence its restriction is a Kegelspitze isomorphism.
∎

Proposition B.0.7
.

For any set
X
, there exists an isomorphism of Kegelspitzen
r_{X}\colon\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X))\cong\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,\mathcal{L}X)\colon r_{X}^{-1}
.
Moreover,
r_{X}
restricts to an isomorphism of sets
r_{X}\colon\mathbf{Q}_{*}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X))\cong\mathbf{TD}(1,\mathcal{L}X)\colon r_{X}^{-1}
.

Proof.

Firstly, we have an isomorphism

\alpha:\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,\mathcal{L}X)=\mathbf{DCPO}(1,\mathcal{ML}X)\to\mathcal{ML}(X)=\mathcal{D}(X),\qquad f\mapsto f(1).

Furthermore, we have an isomorphism
i:\mathbb{C}\to\ell^{\infty}(1)
that to each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
assigns the function
c_{\lambda}\in\ell^{\infty}(1)
defined by
c_{\lambda}(*)=\lambda
. This isomorphism induces an isomorphism

\iota:\mathbf{vN}(\ell^{\infty}(X),\mathbb{C})\to\mathbf{vN}(\ell^{\infty}(X),\ell^{\infty}(1))=\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X)),\qquad f\mapsto i\circ f.

It follows that the required isomorphism
r_{X}^{-1}:\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,\mathcal{L}X)\to\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X))
is given by
\iota\circ\zeta\circ\alpha
, where
\zeta:\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathbf{vN}(\ell^{\infty}(X),\mathbb{C})
is the isomorphism from Proposition
B.0.7
.

Recall that
\ell^{\infty}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Q}_{*}
is a functor if for each function
f:X\to Y
between sets, we define
\ell^{\infty}(f):\ell^{\infty}(Y)\to\ell^{\infty}(X)
by
g\mapsto g\circ f
. By
(
Kornell 2020
, Theorem 7.4)
, this functor is fully faithful. Now consider the following diagram:

\mathbf{Set}(1,X)
\mathbf{DCPO}(1,\mathcal{L}X)
\mathbf{TD}(1,\mathcal{L}X)
\mathcal{J}
\mathbf{Q}_{*}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X))
\ell^{\infty}
\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X))
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,\mathcal{L}X)
r_{X}

Since it consists only of isomorphisms and two injective functors (note that
\mathcal{J}
, which was introduced in Section
6.1
, is fully faithful by construction of
\mathbf{TD}
in Definition
6.1.1
), commutativity of the diagram implies that
r_{X}
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathbf{Q}_{*}(\ell^{\infty}(1),\ell^{\infty}(X))\to\mathbf{TD}(1,\mathcal{L}X)
. In order to show that the diagram indeed is commutative, let
f\in\mathbf{Set}(1,X)
. We will write
f(*)=x
. Then
\ell^{\infty}(f)\in\mathbf{Q}_{*}(\ell^{1}(X),\ell^{\infty}(X))=\mathbf{HA}_{*}(\ell^{\infty}(X),\ell^{\infty}(1))
is given by
\ell^{\infty}(f)(g)=g\circ f
, i.e,
(\ell^{\infty}(f)(g))(*)=(g\circ f)(*)=g(x)
, hence
\ell^{\infty}(f)(g)=c_{g(x)}
.

In the other direction, we have
\mathcal{J}(f)(*)=(\eta_{\mathcal{L}X}\circ f)(*)=\eta_{\mathcal{L}X}(x)=\delta_{x}
, hence

(r_{X}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{J}(f))=(\iota\circ\zeta\circ\alpha)(\mathcal{J}(f))=(\iota\circ\zeta)(\mathcal{J}(f)(*))=(\iota\circ\zeta)(\delta_{x}),

which is a function
\ell^{\infty}(X)\to\ell^{\infty}(1)
, hence for
g\in\ell^{\infty}(X)
, we have

(r_{X}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{J}(f))(g)=(\iota\circ\zeta)(\delta_{x})(g)=\iota\left(\sum_{y\in X}\delta_{x}(y)g(y)\right)=\iota(g(x))=i(g(x))=c_{g(x)}.

Thus
(r_{X}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{J}(f))(g)=c_{g(x)}=\ell^{\infty}(f)(g)
, so
\mathcal{J}(f)=r_{X}\circ\ell^{\infty}(f)
, which shows that the diagram indeed commutes.
∎

Theorem
5.2.4
now follows immediately, because
\mathcal{M}(X,\sqsubseteq)\cong\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,\mathcal{L}X)
and
\mathbf{TD}(1,\mathcal{L}X)
consists precisely of the Dirac valuations of
\mathcal{M}(X,\sqsubseteq).

Appendix C
Construction of the lift isomorphism

We start by proving a general proposition whose second statement is a coherence property used in the proof of soundness/adequacy.

Proposition C.0.1
.

Given a dcpo
X
, hereditarily atomic von Neumann algebras
A,B
, and a
discrete
dcpo
Y
(equivalently, a set), there exists a natural Scott-continuous and linear bijection

\displaystyle\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathbf{DCPO}(X\times Y,\mathbf{Q}(A,B))\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{DCPO}(X,\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(Y)\otimes A,B)).

Furthermore, if
f\in\mathbf{DCPO}(X\times Y,\mathbf{Q}(A,B))
and
g:X\to[1\to Y]
is a Scott-continuous function, then

\forall x\in X.\widehat{f}(x)\circ(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})=\left(f(x,g(x)(*))\circ\cong\right):\ell^{\infty}(1)\otimes A\to B,

as morphisms in
\mathbf{Q}
, where the unnamed *-isomorphism is
\ell^{\infty}(1)\otimes A\cong A.

Proof.

This is established via the following sequence of Scott-continuous bijections:

\mathbf{DCPO}(X\times Y,\mathbf{Q}(A,B))

(currying)

\mathbf{DCPO}(X,[Y\to\mathbf{Q}(A,B)])

(
Y
is discrete)

\mathbf{DCPO}(X,\prod_{|Y|}\mathbf{Q}(A,B))

(
\mathbf{Q}
has
\mathbf{DCPO}
-enriched coproducts)

\mathbf{DCPO}(X,\mathbf{Q}(\coprod_{|Y|}A,B))

\mathbf{DCPO}(X,\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(Y)\otimes A,B))

where
|Y|
indicates the cardinality of
Y
.
Here the last isomorphism is induced by an isomorphism
\ell^{\infty}(Y)\otimes A\to\coprod_{|Y|}A
in
\mathbf{Q}
(and in
\mathbf{Q}_{*}
, which has the same isomorphisms as
\mathbf{Q}
) as follows.
From Proposition
4.1.4
recall that in
\mathbf{HA}_{*}
the
|Y|
-fold product of
A
is given by
\prod_{|Y|}A=\{(a_{y})_{y\in Y}:\sup_{y\in Y}\|a_{y}\|<\infty\}
, from which it easily follows that
\prod_{|Y|}\mathbb{C}=\ell^{\infty}(Y)
. Since
\mathbf{Q}_{*}
is monoidal closed, coproducts commute with the monoidal product
\otimes
, hence in
\mathbf{HA}_{*}
products commute with the spatial tensor product
\bar{\otimes}
, i.e.,

\prod_{|Y|}A\cong\prod_{|Y|}(\mathbb{C}\bar{\otimes}A)\cong\left(\prod_{|Y|}\mathbb{C}\right)\bar{\otimes}A=\ell^{\infty}(Y)\bar{\otimes}A.

The resulting
*
-isomorphism
\theta:\ell^{\infty}(Y)\bar{\otimes}A\to\prod_{|Y|}A
is given explicitly by
\theta(a)=\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}\otimes a_{y}
for each
a=(a_{y})_{y\in Y}
in
\prod_{|Y|}A
, where
e_{y}\in\ell^{\infty}(Y)
is the function
Y\to\mathbb{C}
whose
y
-component is
1
, while all other components vanish. The sum converges in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology.
Let
f\in\mathbf{DCPO}(X\times Y,\mathbf{Q}(A,B))
, so
f
is a Scott continuous function
X\times Y\to\mathbf{HA}(B,A)
. We can now construct
\widehat{f}
as follows. Fix
x\in X
. Then we obtain a function
f(x,-):Y\to\mathbf{HA}(B,A)
, hence for
b\in B
, we have
f(x,-)(b):Y\to A
. This corresponds with an element
(f(x,y)(b))_{y\in Y}
in
\prod_{|Y|}A
, hence using
\theta
, to an element
\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}\otimes f(x,y)(b)
in
\ell^{\infty}(Y)\bar{\otimes}A
. Thus
\widehat{f}(x)(b)=\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}\otimes f(x,y)(b)
.

Now let
g\in\mathbf{DCPO}(X,[1\to Y])
. Then
g(x)
is a function
1\to Y
,
hence
\ell^{\infty}(g(x))
is a morphism
\ell^{\infty}(1)\to\ell^{\infty}(Y)
in
\mathbf{Q}
, corresponding to the
*
-homomorphism
\ell^{\infty}(Y)\to\ell^{\infty}(1)
,
k\mapsto k\circ(g(x))
in
\mathbf{HA}_{*}
.
Thus we obtain a morphism

\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A},B):\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(Y)\otimes A,B)\to\mathbf{Q}(\ell^{\infty}(1)\otimes A,B),\qquad h\mapsto h\circ(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}).

In particular, it follows from choosing
h=\widehat{f}(x)
that
\widehat{f}(x)\circ(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})
is a morphism
\ell^{\infty}(1)\otimes A\to B
in
\mathbf{Q}
, which in the opposite category
\mathbf{HA}
corresponds to the map

(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\circ\widehat{f}(x):B\to\ell^{\infty}(1)\otimes A.

For fixed
b\in B
, we then have

(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\circ\widehat{f}(x)(b)=(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\circ\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}\otimes f(x,y)(b)=\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}(g(x))\otimes f(x,y)(b),

where we used that
\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}
is continuous with respect to the
\sigma
-weak operator topology. Now, the inverse of the isomorphism
\cong:\ell^{\infty}(1)\otimes A\to A
in
\mathbf{Q}
corresponds to the
*
-isomorphism
\zeta:\ell^{\infty}(1)\bar{\otimes}A\to A
in
\mathbf{HA}
, which acts on elementary tensors by
k\otimes a\mapsto k(*)a
. By continuity of
\zeta
with respect to the
\sigma
-weak operator topology, we obtain

\zeta\circ(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\circ\widehat{f}(x)(b)=\sum_{y\in Y}e_{y}(g(x)(*))f(x,y)(b)=\sum_{y\in Y}\delta_{y,g(x)(*)}f(x,y)(b)=f(x,g(x)(*))(b).

Thus
(\ell^{\infty}(g(x))\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})\circ\widehat{f}(x)=\zeta^{-1}\circ f(x,g(x)(*))
in
\mathbf{HA}
, whence the expression in the statement holds in the opposite category
\mathbf{Q}
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Strong Adequacy

In this appendix we provide a proof of Theorem
7.4.9
. We begin by stating a corollary for the soundness theorem.

Corollary D.0.1
.

Let
\cdot\vdash m:P
be a closed classical term and
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
a closed quantum configuration. Then:

\displaystyle{\llbracket m\rrbracket}\geq\sum_{v\in\text{Val}(m)}P(m\xrightarrow{}_{*}v){\llbracket v\rrbracket}\qquad\qquad{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}\geq\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{ValC}(\mathcal{C})}P(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}_{*}\mathcal{V}){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}.

Proof.

The classical statement is identical to
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Corollary 67)
and the quantum statement is fully analogous.
∎

The remainder of the appendix is dedicated to showing the converse inequalities, which are much more difficult to prove.

D.1.
Overview of the Proof Strategy

Our proof strategy uses logical relations to establish strong adequacy. Our
logical relations are described in Theorem
D.8.44
and their
design follows that of
(
Jia
et al. 2021
)
which is in turn based on the logical
relations of Claire Jones in her thesis
(
Jones 1990
)
. We establish some
useful closure properties for these relations in Subsection

D.9
and this allows us to prove the Fundamental Lemma
(Lemma
D.10.72
). Once the Fundamental Lemma is proved, strong
adequacy follows easily.

A large part of the effort in proving Strong Adequacy lies in the proof of
Theorem
D.8.44
. The classical logical relations there are
defined via non-well-founded induction. The proof of the existence of these
relations is not obvious. We use methods from

(
Jia
et al. 2021
;
Lindenhovius et al. 2019
;
Lindenhovius et al. 2021
)
(which are in turn based on ideas from

(
Fiore 1994
)
) to show the existence of these logical relations.

The quantum logical relations are actually easier to define and we do this
first (Subsection
D.3
). The reason for this is that the
quantum subsystem is
first-order
and all the
quantum values

depend only on themselves and not on classical terms or quantum terms. This is
not the case for the classical values, because they may depend on terms that
are not values (e.g. lambda abstractions). Existence of the quantum logical
relations is clear and immediate from their definition.

The main idea for the proof of existence of the classical relations is the
following. For every type
P
, we define a category
\mathbf{R}(P)
of logical
relations with a suitable notion of morphism. We show that every such category
has sufficient structure to construct parameterised initial algebras
(Proposition
D.6.27
). It follows that we may define
functors on these categories (Proposition
D.6.33
) which
construct logical relations as they are needed in Theorem

D.8.44
. All of these functors are
\omega
-cocontinuous
(Proposition
D.6.34
) and therefore we may form
(parameterised) initial algebras using them. This, in turn, allows us to define

augmented interpretation of types
on the categories
\mathbf{R}(P)
. These
interpretations satisfy important coherence conditions with respect to the
standard interpretation of types (Corollary
D.7.42
). These
coherence conditions are important, because they show that every augmented
interpretation
{\lVert P\rVert}
of a type
P
contains the standard interpretation

{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
, together with the logical relation that we need for the proof, as
shown in Theorem
D.8.44
.

The proof stategy that we use to define the classical logical relations and
their existence is heavily based on
(
Jia
et al. 2021
)
, so we use the same notation
as there.

D.2.
Notation for Reduction Paths

Before we may define our logical relations, we have to introduce some auxiliary definitions for reduction paths.

Assumption D.2.2
.

Throughout this appendix, we assume that all types are closed, unless otherwise noted.

Definition D.2.3.

For each classical type
P
and quantum type
\mathbf{A}
we write:

•

\mathrm{Val}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\{V\ |\ V\text{ is a classical value and }\cdot\vdash V:P\}.

•

\mathrm{Prog}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\{M\ |\ M\text{ is a classical term and }\cdot\vdash M:P\}.

•

\mathrm{ValC}(\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\{\mathcal{V}\ |\ \mathcal{V}\text{ is a value configuration and }\cdot\vdash\mathcal{V}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}\}.

•

\mathrm{C}(\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\{\mathcal{C}\ |\ \mathcal{C}\text{ is a configuration and }\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}\}.

Definition D.2.4.

Let
M\colon P
and
N\colon P
be closed classical terms of the same type. We define

\mathrm{Paths(M,N)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\pi\ |\ \pi=\left(M=M_{0}\xrightarrow{p_{0}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}M_{2}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{p_{n}}M_{n}=N\right)\text{ is a reduction path}\right\}.

In other words,
\mathrm{Paths(M,N)}
is the set of all reduction paths from
M
to
N
. The
probability weight
of a path
\pi\in\mathrm{Paths}(M,N)
is
P(\pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\prod_{i=0}^{n}p_{i},
i.e., it is simply the product of all the probabilities of single-step reductions within the path.
The
set of terminal reduction paths of
M
is

\mathrm{\mathrm{TPaths}(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{V\in\mathrm{Val}(P)}\mathrm{Paths}(M,V).

Thus the endpoint of any path
\pi\in\mathrm{TPaths}(M)
is a value. If
\pi\in\mathrm{Paths}(M,W)
, where
W
is a value, then we shall write
V_{\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}W.
That is, for a path
\pi\in\mathrm{TPaths}(M)
, the notation
V_{\pi}
indicates the endpoint of the path
\pi
which is indeed a value.

Reduction paths for quantum configurations are defined in the same way.

Definition D.2.5.

Let
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}\colon\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
and
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{D}\colon\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
be closed quantum configurations of the same type. We define

\mathrm{Paths(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\pi\ |\ \pi=\left(\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{2}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{p_{n}}\mathcal{C}_{n}=\mathcal{D}\right)\text{ is a reduction path}\right\}.

In other words,
\mathrm{Paths(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}
is the set of all reduction paths from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
. The
probability weight
of a path
\pi\in\mathrm{Paths}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is
P(\pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\prod_{i=0}^{n}p_{i},
.
The
set of terminal reduction paths of
\mathcal{C}
is

\mathrm{\mathrm{TPaths}(\mathcal{C})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{ValC}(\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k})}\mathrm{Paths}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{V}).

Thus the endpoint of any path
\pi\in\mathrm{TPaths}(\mathcal{C})
is a value configuration. If
\pi\in\mathrm{Paths}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W})
, where
\mathcal{W}
is a value configuration, then we shall write
\mathcal{V}_{\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}.
That is, for a path
\pi\in\mathrm{TPaths}(\mathcal{C})
, the notation
\mathcal{V}_{\pi}

indicates the endpoint of the path
\pi
which is indeed a value configuration.

Remark D.2.6
.

We also note that for each closed classical term
M
and each closed quantum configuration
\mathcal{C}
the sets
\mathrm{TPaths}(M)
and
\mathrm{TPaths}(\mathcal{C})
are both countable.

D.3.
The Quantum Logical Relation

Next, we define a logical relation between quantum configurations and their semantic domain.

Definition D.3.7.

For each closed quantum type
\mathbf{A}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a
logical relation

\displaystyle\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{Q}(\mathbb{C},{\llbracket\mathbf{A}\otimes\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}\rrbracket})\times\mathrm{C}(\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k})\qquad\text{by}

\displaystyle c\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\text{ iff }c\in\mathcal{S}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C}),\text{ where }\mathcal{S}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C})\text{ is the Scott-closure in $\mathbf{Q}(\mathbb{C},{\llbracket\mathbf{A}\otimes\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}\rrbracket})$ of the set }

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in F}P(\pi){\llbracket\mathcal{V}_{\pi}\rrbracket}\ |\ F\subseteq\mathrm{TPaths}(\mathcal{C}),\ F\text{ is finite}\right\}.

Lemma D.3.8
.

If
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{V}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
is a value configuration, then
{\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft^{k}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{V}.

Proof.

This is immediate by Definition
D.3.7
.
∎

D.4.
Classical Logical Relations

Assumption D.4.9
.

In this section and the next two, all types are assumed to be classical, unless otherwise noted.

We define sets of relations that are parameterised by dcpo’s
X
from our semantic category, types
P
from our language and partial deterministic embeddings
e_{X}:X\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
which show how
X
approximates
{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
.
We shall write relation membership in infix notation, that is, for a binary relation
\vartriangleleft
, we write
v\vartriangleleft V
to indicate
(v,V)\in\vartriangleleft.

Definition D.4.10.

For any dcpo
X
, type
P
and morphism
e\colon X\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
in
\mathbf{PD}_{e}
, let:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)

\displaystyle=\{\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\subseteq\mathbf{TD}(1,X)\times\mathrm{Val}(P)\ |\ \forall V\in\mathrm{Val}(P).\ (-)\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}V\text{ is a Scott closed subset of }

\displaystyle\mathbf{TD}(1,X)\text{ and}\forall V\in\mathrm{Val}(P).\ v\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}V\Rightarrow e\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\leq{\llbracket V\rrbracket}\}.

Remark D.4.11
.

In the above definition, relations
\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)
can be seen as ternary relations
\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\subseteq\mathbf{TD}(1,X)\times\mathrm{Val}(P)\times\{e\}
. However, since there is no choice for the third component, we prefer to see them as binary relations that are parameterised by the embeddings
e
. Indeed, this leads to a much nicer notation.
We shall also sometimes indicate the parameters
X,P
and
e
of the relation in order to avoid confusion as to which set
\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)
it belongs to.

The relations we need for the adequacy proof inhabit the sets
\mathrm{ValRel}({\llbracket P\rrbracket},P,\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket P\rrbracket}})
. In the remainder of the appendix, we will show how to choose exactly one relation (the one we need) from each of those sets.

The next definition we introduce is crucial for the proof of strong adequacy.

Definition D.4.12.

Given a relation
\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)
and a term
\cdot\vdash M:P
, let
\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e};M)
be the Scott-closure in
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,X)
of the set

(D.1)

\mathcal{S}_{0}(\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e};M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in F}P(\pi)v_{\pi}\ |\ F\subseteq\mathrm{TPaths}(M),\ F\text{ is finite and }\text{$v_{\pi}\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}V_{\pi}$ for each $\pi\in F$}\right\}.

In other words,
\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e};M)
is the smallest Scott-closed subset of
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,X)
which contains all morphisms of the form in (
D.1
).
For a subset
U\subseteq\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,X),
we write
\overline{U}
to indicate its Scott-closure in
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,X)
.

Lemma D.4.13
.

For any value
V
, we have
\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e};V)=\ \overline{\{v\ |\ v\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}V\}}\ \cup\{0\}=\ \overline{\{v\ |\ v\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}V\}\cup\{0\}}\ .

Proof.

This is because all of the sums in (
D.1
) are singleton sums or the empty sum.
∎

Lemma D.4.14

(
(
Jones 1990
, Lemma 8.4)
)
.

Let
Y
be a dcpo and let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in F}
be a finite collection of dcpo’s.
Let
f\colon\prod_{i}X_{i}\to Y
be a Scott-continuous function. Let
C_{Y}
be a Scott-closed subset of
Y
. Let
U_{i}\subseteq X_{i}
be arbitrary subsets, such that
f(\prod_{i}U_{i})\subseteq C_{Y}
.
Then
f(\prod_{i}\overline{U_{i}})\subseteq C_{Y}
, where
\overline{U_{i}}
is the Scott-closure of
U_{i}
in
X_{i}
.

Lemma D.4.15

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 77)
)
.

Let
\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P}^{e_{1}}
and
\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P}^{e_{2}}
be two logical relations and
\cdot\vdash M:P
a term.
Assume that
g:X_{1}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}X_{2}
is a morphism, such that
v\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P}^{e_{1}}V
implies
g\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\in\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P}^{e_{2}};V),
for any
V\in\mathrm{Val}(M).

If
m\in\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P}^{e_{1}};M)
, then
g\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\in\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P}^{e_{2}};M).

Next, we define important
closure relations
which we use for terms.

Definition D.4.16.

If
\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)
, let
{\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}}\ }\subseteq\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,X)\times\mathrm{Prog}(P)
be the relation defined by

m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}}\ M\text{ iff }m\in\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e};M).

Lemma D.4.17
.

For any term
\cdot\vdash M:P
and
\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)
, the set
(-)\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}}\ M
is a Scott-closed subset of
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,X).

Proof.

This follows immediately by definition, because
\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e};M)
is Scott-closed.
∎

Lemma D.4.18

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 80)
)
.

Let
C
be a Scott-closed subset of a dcpo
X
. Let
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\{\delta_{x}\ |\ x\in C\}\subseteq\mathcal{M}X
and let
\overline{W}
be the Scott-closure of
W
in
\mathcal{M}X.
Then,
\delta_{y}\in\overline{W}
iff
y\in C.

Lemma D.4.19

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 81)
)
.

Let
X
be a dcpo, let
v\in\mathbf{TD}(1,X)
and let
V
be a value. Then
v\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}V
iff
v\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}}\ V.

Lemma D.4.20

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 82)
)
.

For any value
\cdot\vdash V:P
and
\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e)
, if
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X,P}^{e}}\ V
then
e\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\leq{\llbracket V\rrbracket}
.

D.5.
Logical Relations for types 1 and
Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})

The unit type
1
and the type of quantum functions
Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
have simple
type structures, because they do not depend on any other classical types. As a
result, it is easy to define the required logical relations at those types and
we do so now.

Definition D.5.21.

We define a logical relation
\vartriangleleft_{1}\in\mathrm{ValRel}({\llbracket 1\rrbracket},1,\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket 1\rrbracket}})
by:

f\vartriangleleft_{1}()\ \text{iff}\ f=\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket 1\rrbracket}}.

Definition D.5.22.

For every two closed quantum types
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, we define a logical relation
\vartriangleleft_{Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})}\in\mathrm{ValRel}({\llbracket Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\rrbracket},Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}),\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\rrbracket}})
by:

f\vartriangleleft_{Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})}\mathbf{f}\text{ iff }f\leq{\llbracket\mathbf{f}\rrbracket}\text{ and }\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\forall(\cdot\vdash[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}]:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}).

(\beta(f(*))\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{\llbracket[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}]\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{B}}^{k}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{f}\mathbf{v}],

where the second quantifier ranges over well-formed
value confiugrations
of the indicated type.

It is easy to see that both logical relations are well-defined.
Furthermore, notice that the second family of logical relations is defined via the quantum logical relations on configurations.

D.6.
Categories of Logical Relations

Definition D.6.23.

For any type
P
, we define a category
\mathbf{R}(P)
where:

•

Each object is a triple
(X,e_{X},\vartriangleleft_{X})
, where
X
is a dcpo,
e_{X}\colon X\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{PD}_{e}
and
\vartriangleleft_{X}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P,e_{X})
.

•

A morphism
f:(X,e_{X},\vartriangleleft_{X})\to(Y,e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{Y})
is a morphism
f:X\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}Y
in
\mathbf{PD}_{e}
, which satisfies the three additional conditions:

–

If
v\vartriangleleft_{X}V,
then
f\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{Y}}\ V.

–

If
v\vartriangleleft_{Y}V,
then
f^{p}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X}}\ V.

–

e_{X}=e_{Y}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}f.

•

Composition and identities coincide with those in
\mathbf{PD}_{e}.

Lemma D.6.24

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 84)
)
.

For every type
P
, the category
\mathbf{R}(P)
is indeed well-defined.

Lemma D.6.25
.

Let
\cdot\vdash M:P
be a term and let
g\colon(X,e_{X},\vartriangleleft_{X})\to(Y,e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{Y})
be a morphism in
\mathbf{R}(P)
. If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X}}\ M
then
g\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{Y}}\ M
.
Moreover, if
n\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{Y}}\ N,
then
g^{p}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}n\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X}}\
N.

Proof.

This follows immediately by Lemma
D.4.15
.
∎

Definition D.6.26.

For every type
P
, we define the obvious forgetful functor
U^{P}\colon\mathbf{R}(P)\to\mathbf{PD}_{e}
by

\displaystyle U^{P}(X,e,\vartriangleleft)

\displaystyle=X

\displaystyle U^{P}(f)

\displaystyle=f.

Proposition D.6.27

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Proposition 87)
)
.

For each type
P
, the category
\mathbf{R}(P)
has an initial object and all
\omega
-colimits. Furthermore, the forgetful functor
U^{P}\colon\mathbf{R}(P)\to\mathbf{PD}_{e}
preserves and reflects
\omega
-colimits (and also the initial objects).

Next, we introduce important relation constructors and some new notation.

Notation D.6.28.

Given morphisms
m_{i}:1\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}X_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},
we define

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}(m_{1}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern-0.21529pt\cr$\times$\cr}}}\cdots\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern-0.21529pt\cr$\times$\cr}}}m_{n})\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}\mathcal{J}\langle\mathrm{id}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{id}_{1}\rangle:1\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}.

Notation D.6.29.

Given morphisms
x:1\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}X
and
f:1\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}[X\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}Y]
in
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}
, let
f[x]:1\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}Y
be the morphism defined by

f[x]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\epsilon\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}(f\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern-0.21529pt\cr$\times$\cr}}}x)\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}\mathcal{J}\langle\mathrm{id}_{1},\mathrm{id}_{1}\rangle.

Definition D.6.30 (Relation Constructions).

We define relation constructors:

•

If
\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{1},P_{1},e_{1})
and
\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{2},P_{2},e_{2})
, define

\displaystyle(\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}

\displaystyle+\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}})\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{1}+X_{2},P_{1}+P_{2},e_{1}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.86108pt\cr$+$\cr}}}e_{2})\text{ by: }

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\emph{in}_{i}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\ (\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}

\displaystyle+\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}})\ \mathtt{in}_{i}V\text{ iff }v\vartriangleleft_{X_{i},P_{i}}^{e_{i}}V\qquad\qquad(\text{for $i\in\{1,2\}$}).

•

If
\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{1},P_{1},e_{1})
and
\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{2},P_{2},e_{2})
, define

\displaystyle(\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}})\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{1}\times X_{2},P_{1}\times P_{2},e_{1}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern-0.21529pt\cr$\times$\cr}}}e_{2})\text{ by: }

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{1},v_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\ (\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}})\ (V_{1},V_{2})\text{ iff }v_{1}\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}V_{1}\text{ and }v_{2}\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}}V_{2}.

•

If
\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{1},P_{1},e_{1})
and
\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X_{2},P_{2},e_{2})
, define

\displaystyle(\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}

\displaystyle\to\ \vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}})\in\mathrm{ValRel}([X_{1}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}X_{2}],P_{1}\to P_{2},\mathcal{J}[e_{1}^{p}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}e_{2}])\text{ by: }

\displaystyle f\ (\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}

\displaystyle\to\ \vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}})\ \lambda x.M\text{ iff }\mathcal{J}[e_{1}^{p}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}e_{2}]\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}f\leq{\llbracket\lambda x.M\rrbracket}\text{ and }\forall(v\vartriangleleft_{X_{1},P_{1}}^{e_{1}}V).\ f[v]\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{X_{2},P_{2}}^{e_{2}}}\ (\lambda x.M)V.

Lemma D.6.31

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 91)
)
.

The assignments in Definition
D.6.30
are indeed well-defined.

Notation D.6.32.

Throughout the rest of the paper we shall write
(-\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}_{e}-)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}[-\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}-]_{e}^{\mathcal{J}}:\mathbf{PD}_{e}\times\mathbf{PD}_{e}\to\mathbf{PD}_{e}
. That is, we just introduce a more concise notation for the functor
[-\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}-]_{e}^{\mathcal{J}}
from Proposition
6.1.6
.

The next definition is crucial. Given two logical relations, it is used to define the product, coproduct and function space logical relations. Moreover, this is done in a functorial sense on the categories
\mathbf{R}(P)
.

Proposition D.6.33

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Proposition 95)
)
.

Let
P
and
R
be types. The following assignments (recall Definition
D.6.30
):

(1)

\times^{P,R}\colon\mathbf{R}(P)\times\mathbf{R}(R)\to\mathbf{R}(P\times R)
by

\displaystyle(X,e_{X},\vartriangleleft_{X})\times^{P,R}(Y,e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{Y})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}(X\times Y,e_{X}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern-0.21529pt\cr$\times$\cr}}}_{e}e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{X}\times\vartriangleleft_{Y})

\displaystyle f\times^{P,R}g

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}f\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern-0.21529pt\cr$\times$\cr}}}_{e}g

(2)

+^{P,R}\colon\mathbf{R}(P)\times\mathbf{R}(R)\to\mathbf{R}(P+R)
by

\displaystyle(X,e_{X},\vartriangleleft_{X})+^{P,R}(Y,e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{Y})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}(X+Y,e_{X}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.86108pt\cr$+$\cr}}}_{e}e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{X}+\vartriangleleft_{Y})

\displaystyle f+^{P,R}g

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}f\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.86108pt\cr$+$\cr}}}_{e}g

(3)

\to^{P,R}\colon\mathbf{R}(P)\times\mathbf{R}(R)\to\mathbf{R}(P\to R)
by

\displaystyle(X,e_{X},\vartriangleleft_{X})\to^{P,R}(Y,e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{Y})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}([X\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}Y],e_{X}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}_{e}e_{Y},\vartriangleleft_{X}\to\vartriangleleft_{Y})

\displaystyle f\to^{P,R}g

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}f\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}_{e}g

define
covariant
functors with the indicated types.

Observe that Proposition
D.6.33
lifts the functors that we use to interpret our types in the category
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}
to the categories
\mathbf{R}(P)
. Next, we show that the functors we just defined are also suitable for forming (parameterised) initial algebras.

Proposition D.6.34

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Proposition 96)
)
.

For
\star\in\{\times,+,\to\},
for all types
P
and
R
, the functor
\star^{P,R}:\mathbf{R}(P)\times\mathbf{R}(R)\to\mathbf{R}(P\star R)
is
\omega
-cocontinuous and the following diagram:

\mathbf{R}(P)\times\mathbf{R}(R)
\mathbf{R}(P\star R)
\star^{P,R}
\mathbf{PD}_{e}\times\mathbf{PD}_{e}
\mathbf{PD}_{e}
\mathbin{\vbox{\scalebox{1}{.}\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr$\star$\cr}}_{e}
U^{P\star R}
U^{P}\times U^{R}

commutes.

Next, we establish an isomorphism between the categories
\mathbf{R}(\mu X.P)
and
\mathbf{R}(P[\mu X.P/X]).

Definition D.6.35.

We define constructors for folding and unfolding logical relations as follows:

•

If
\vartriangleleft_{X,P[\mu Y.P/Y]}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P[\mu Y.P/Y],e)
, define

\displaystyle(\mathbb{I}^{\mu Y.P}

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft_{X,P[\mu Y.P/Y]}^{e})\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,\mu Y.P,\mathrm{fold}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}e)\text{ by:}

\displaystyle v\ (\mathbb{I}^{\mu Y.P}

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft_{X,P[\mu Y.P/Y]}^{e})\ \mathtt{fold}\ V\text{ iff }v\vartriangleleft_{X,P[\mu Y.P/Y]}^{e}V.

•

If
\vartriangleleft_{X,\mu Y.P}^{e}\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,\mu Y.P,e)
, define

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}^{\mu Y.P}

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft_{X,\mu Y.P}^{e})\in\mathrm{ValRel}(X,P[\mu Y.P/Y],\mathrm{unfold}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}e)\text{ by:}

\displaystyle v\ (\mathbb{E}^{\mu Y.P}

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft_{X,\mu Y.P}^{e}))\ V\text{ iff }v\vartriangleleft_{X,\mu Y.P}^{e}\mathtt{fold}\ V.

Proposition D.6.36

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Proposition 99)
)
.

For every type
\cdot\vdash\mu X.P,
we have an isomorphism of categories

\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P}:\mathbf{R}(P[\mu X.P/X])\cong\mathbf{R}(\mu X.P):\mathbb{E}^{\mu X.P},

where the functors are defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P}:\mathbf{R}(P[\mu X.P/X])\to\mathbf{R}(\mu X.P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mu X.P}:\mathbf{R}(\mu X.P)\to\mathbf{R}(P[\mu X.P/X])

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P}(Y,e,\vartriangleleft)=(Y,\mathrm{fold}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}e,\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P}\vartriangleleft)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mu X.P}(Y,e,\vartriangleleft)=(Y,\mathrm{unfold}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}e,\mathbb{E}^{\mu X.P}\vartriangleleft)

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P}(f)=f

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mu X.P}(f)=f.

This finishes the categorical development of the categories
\mathbf{R}(P)
.

D.7.
Augmented Interpretation of Types

We have now established sufficient categorical structure in order to construct parameterised initial algebras in the categories
\mathbf{R}(P).
Furthermore, we have sufficient structure to also define an
augmented
interpretation of types in these categories.
The main idea behind providing the augmented interpretation is to show how to pick out the logical relations we need from all those that exist in the categories
\mathbf{R}(P)
.

Notation D.7.37.

Given any type context
\Theta=X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and closed types
\cdot\vdash C_{i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
we shall write
\vec{C}
for
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
and we also write
[\vec{C}/\Theta]
for
[C_{1}/X_{1},\ldots,C_{n}/X_{n}]
.

Definition D.7.38.

For any type
\Theta\vdash P
and closed types
\vec{C}
, we define their
augmented interpretation
to be the functor

{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}:\mathbf{R}(C_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathbf{R}(C_{n})\to\mathbf{R}(P[\vec{C}/\Theta])

defined by induction on the derivation of
\Theta\vdash P
:

\displaystyle{{\lVert\Theta\vdash\Theta_{i}\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=\Pi_{i}

\displaystyle{{\lVert\Theta\vdash 1\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=K_{({\llbracket 1\rrbracket},\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket 1\rrbracket}},\vartriangleleft_{1})}

\displaystyle{{\lVert\Theta\vdash Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=K_{({\llbracket Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\rrbracket},\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\rrbracket}},\vartriangleleft_{Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})})}

\displaystyle{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\star R\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=\star^{P[\vec{C}/\Theta],R[\vec{C}/\Theta]}\circ\langle{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}},{{\lVert\Theta\vdash R\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}\rangle

\displaystyle(\text{for }\star\in\{+,\times,\to\})

\displaystyle{{\lVert\Theta\vdash\mu X.P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P[\vec{C}/\Theta]}\circ{\lVert\Theta,X\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C},\mu X.P[\vec{C}/\Theta]}}\right)^{\sharp},

where
K_{Y}
is the constant functor on
Y
and the
(-)^{\sharp}
operation is from Definition
6.1.3
.

Proposition D.7.39
.

Each functor
{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}
is well-defined and
\omega
-cocontinuous. Moreover, the following diagram:

\mathbf{R}(C_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathbf{R}(C_{n})
\mathbf{R}(P[\vec{C}/\Theta])
{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}
\mathbf{PD}_{e}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{PD}_{e}
\mathbf{PD}_{e}
{\llbracket\Theta\vdash P\rrbracket}
U^{P[\vec{C}/\Theta]}
U^{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times U^{C_{n}}

commutes.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as
(
Lindenhovius et al. 2021
, Proposition 7.26)
.
∎

Next, a corollary which shows that parameterised initial algebras for our type expressions are constructed in the same way in both categories.

Corollary D.7.40
.

The 2-categorical diagram:

\mathbf{R}(C_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathbf{R}(C_{n})
\mathbf{R}(P[\vec{C}/\Theta])
\iota
\mathbf{PD}_{e}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{PD}_{e}
\mathbf{PD}_{e}
{\llbracket\Theta,X\vdash P\rrbracket}\circ\langle\mathrm{Id},{\llbracket\Theta\vdash\mu X.P\rrbracket}\rangle
\iota
{{\lVert\Theta\vdash\mu X.P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}
{\llbracket\Theta\vdash\mu X.P\rrbracket}
U^{P[\vec{C}/\Theta]}
U^{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times U^{C_{n}}
\mathbb{I}^{\mu X.P[\vec{C}/\Theta]}\circ{\lVert\Theta,X\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C},\mu X.P[\vec{C}/\Theta]}}\circ\langle\mathrm{Id},{{\lVert\Theta\vdash\mu X.P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}\rangle

commutes, where
\iota
is the parameterised initial algebra isomorphism (see Definition
6.1.3
).

Proof.

The proof is the same as
(
Lindenhovius et al. 2021
, Corollary 7.27)
.
∎

Proposition
D.7.39
shows that the first component of the augmented interpretation coincides with the standard interpretation. This is true for all types, including open ones.
In the special case for closed types, let
{\lVert P\rVert}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}{\lVert\cdot\vdash P\rVert^{\cdot}}(*)
, where
*
is the unique object of the terminal category
\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{R}(P)^{0}
.
Proposition
D.7.39
therefore shows that
U{\lVert P\rVert}={\llbracket P\rrbracket}
, which means that
{\lVert P\rVert}
has the form
{\lVert P\rVert}=({\llbracket P\rrbracket},e,\vartriangleleft),
where
e:{\llbracket P\rrbracket}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
is some
embedding. Next, we show that
e=\textbf{id}.
In order to do this, we prove a stronger proposition first. We show that the action of the functor
{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}
on the embedding component is also completely determined by the action of
{\llbracket\Theta\vdash P\rrbracket}
on embeddings.

Proposition D.7.41
.

For every functor
{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}
and objects
(X_{i},e_{i},\vartriangleleft_{i})
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have:

\pi_{e}\left({{\lVert\Theta\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}\left((X_{1},e_{1},\vartriangleleft_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},e_{n},\vartriangleleft_{n})\right)\right)={\llbracket\Theta\vdash P\rrbracket}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}),

where for an object
(Z,e_{Z},\vartriangleleft_{Z})
in any category
\mathbf{R}(R)
, we define
\pi_{e}(Z,e_{Z},\vartriangleleft_{Z})=e_{Z}.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\Theta\vdash P.

Case

P=1

The functors on both sides are constant ones, so this is a trivial verification.

Case

P=Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})

The functors on both sides are constant ones, so this is a trivial verification.

The remaining cases follow using exactly the same arguments as in
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Proposition 104)
.
∎

Corollary D.7.42
.

For every closed classical type
P
, we have
{\lVert P\rVert}=({\llbracket P\rrbracket},\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket P\rrbracket}},\vartriangleleft_{P})
for some logical relation
\vartriangleleft_{P}.

Proof.

We already know that the first component is
{\llbracket P\rrbracket}
. For the second component, the previous proposition shows that
\pi_{e}{\lVert P\rVert}=\pi_{e}{\lVert\cdot\vdash P\rVert^{\cdot}}(*)={\llbracket\cdot\vdash P\rrbracket}(\mathrm{id}_{*})=\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket P\rrbracket}},
where
*
denotes the empty tuple of objects and
\mathrm{id}_{*}
the empty tuple of embeddings.
∎

Finally, we want to show that the third component of
{\lVert P\rVert}
is the logical relation that we need to carry out the adequacy proof. For this, we have to prove a substitution lemma first.

Lemma D.7.43

(Substitution)
.

For any classical types
\Theta,X\vdash P
and
\Theta\vdash R
and closed types
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
, we have:

{{\lVert\Theta\vdash P[R/X]\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}={\lVert\Theta,X\vdash P\rVert^{\vec{C},R[\vec{C}/\Theta]}}\circ\langle\mathrm{Id},{{\lVert\Theta\vdash R\rVert^{\vec{C}}}}\rangle.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\Theta,X\vdash P
. The cases for the types
1
and
Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
are trivial, because they involve constant functors. The remaining cases can be proven in the same way as
(
Lindenhovius et al. 2021
, Lemma 7.30)
.
∎

For each type
P
, we have now provided an augmented interpretation
{\lVert P\rVert}
of
P
in the category
\mathbf{R}(P).

The interpretation
{\lVert-\rVert}
satisfies all the fundamental properties of
{\llbracket-\rrbracket},
as we have now shown. It should now be clear that this augmented interpretation is true to its name, because it carries strictly more information compared to the standard interpretation of types.
The additional information that
{\lVert P\rVert}
carries is precisely the logical relation that we need at type
P
, as we show in the next subsection.

D.8.
Existence of the Logical Relations

We can now show that the logical relations we need for the adequacy proof exist.

Theorem D.8.44
.

For each closed classical type
P
, there exist
formal approximation relations:

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft_{P}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{TD}(1,{\llbracket P\rrbracket})\times\mathrm{Val}(P)

\displaystyle\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,{\llbracket P\rrbracket})\times\mathrm{Prog}(P)

which satisfy the following properties:

(A0)

v\vartriangleleft_{1}()\text{ iff }v=\textbf{id}_{1}
.

(A1)

\mathcal{J}\emph{in}_{i}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}+P_{2}}\mathtt{in}_{i}V\text{ iff }v\vartriangleleft_{P_{i}}V
, where
i\in\{1,2\}
.

(A2)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}v_{1},v_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}\times P_{2}}(V_{1},V_{2})\text{ iff }v_{1}\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}}V_{1}\text{ and }v_{2}\vartriangleleft_{P_{2}}V_{2}.

(A3)

f\vartriangleleft_{P\to R}\lambda x.M\text{ iff }f\leq{\llbracket\lambda x.M\rrbracket}\text{ and }\forall(v\vartriangleleft_{P}V).\ f[v]\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{R}}\ (\lambda x.M)V.

(A4)

v\vartriangleleft_{\mu X.P}\mathtt{fold}\ V\text{ iff }\mathrm{unfold}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v\vartriangleleft_{P[\mu X.P/X]}V
.

(A5)

f\vartriangleleft_{Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})}\mathbf{f}\text{ iff }f\leq{\llbracket\mathbf{f}\rrbracket}\text{ and }\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\forall(\cdot\vdash[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}]:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}).

(\beta(f(*))\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{\llbracket[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}]\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{B}}^{k}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{f}\mathbf{v}].

(R)

m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M\text{ iff }m\in\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{P};M),
where
\mathcal{S}(\vartriangleleft_{P};M)
is the Scott-closure in
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,{\llbracket P\rrbracket})
of the set

\mathcal{S}_{0}(\vartriangleleft_{P};M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in F}P(\pi)v_{\pi}\ |\ F\subseteq\mathrm{TPaths}(M)\text{ is finite and }\text{$v_{\pi}\vartriangleleft_{P}V_{\pi}$ for each $\pi\in F$}\right\}\ (\text{see Definition \ref{def:paths}}).

(C1)

If
v\vartriangleleft_{P}V
, then
v\leq{\llbracket V\rrbracket}
.

(C2)

(-\vartriangleleft_{P}V)
is a Scott-closed subset of
\mathbf{TD}(1,{\llbracket P\rrbracket}).

(C3)

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M
, then
m\leq{\llbracket M\rrbracket}
.

(C4)

(-\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M)
is a Scott-closed subset of
\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}(1,{\llbracket P\rrbracket}).

(C5)

If
v\in\mathbf{TD}(1,{\llbracket P\rrbracket})
and
V
is a value, then
v\vartriangleleft_{P}V
iff
v\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ V.

Proof.

Consider the object
{\lVert P\rVert}\in\mathbf{R}(P).
We have already shown that
{\lVert P\rVert}=({\llbracket P\rrbracket},\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket P\rrbracket}},\vartriangleleft_{P})
for some logical relation
\vartriangleleft_{P}\in\mathrm{ValRel}({\llbracket P\rrbracket},P,\textbf{id}_{{\llbracket P\rrbracket}}).
We now show that
\vartriangleleft_{P}
satisfies the required properties.
Notice that the embedding components are just identities.

Properties (A0) and (A5) are satisfied by construction. To show the remaining properties are satisfied we simply use the same arguments as in
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Theorem 107)
.

∎

D.9.
Closure Properties of the Logical Relations

Here we establish some important closure properties of our logical relations.

Lemma D.9.45

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 108)
)
.

Let
\cdot\vdash M:P
be a term and let
F
be some finite index set. Assume that we are given morphisms
m_{i}
and terms
M_{i}
such that
m_{i}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M_{i}
for
i\in F
.
Assume further that for each
i\in F
, we are given a reduction path
\pi_{i}\in\mathrm{Paths}(M,M_{i})
, such that all paths
\pi_{i}
are distinct.
Then

\sum_{i\in F}P(\pi_{i})m_{i}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M.

Lemma D.9.46

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 109)
)
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M
and
n\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ N,
then
p\cdot m+(1-p)\cdot n\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ M\ \mathtt{or}_{p}\ N.

Lemma D.9.47

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 110)
)
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}:
if
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{i}}}\ M
, then
\mathcal{J}\emph{\emph{in}}_{i}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}+P_{2}}}\ \mathtt{in}_{i}M.

Lemma D.9.48

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 111)
)
.

Let
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}+P_{2}}}\ M
. Next, assume that for
k\in\{1,2\}
we have terms
x_{k}:P_{k}\vdash N_{k}:R
and morphisms
n_{k}\colon{\llbracket P_{k}\rrbracket}\mathbin{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr\scalebox{2}{.}\cr\kern-3.01385pt\cr$\to$\cr}}}{\llbracket R\rrbracket}
, such that for every
v_{k}\vartriangleleft_{P_{k}}V_{k},
it is the case that
n_{k}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}v_{k}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{R}}\ N_{k}[V_{k}/x_{k}].

Then

[n_{1},n_{2}]\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{R}}\ \mathtt{case}\ M\ \mathtt{of}\ \mathtt{in}_{1}x_{1}\Rightarrow N_{1}\ |\ \mathtt{in}_{2}x_{2}\Rightarrow N_{2}.

Lemma D.9.49

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 112)
)
.

If
m_{1}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}}}\ M_{1}
and
m_{2}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{2}}}\ M_{2}
then
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}m_{1},m_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}\times P_{2}}}\ (M_{1},M_{2}).

Lemma D.9.50

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 113)
)
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}\times P_{2}}}\ M
then
\mathcal{J}\pi_{i}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P_{i}}}\ \pi_{i}M
, for
i\in\{1,2\}.

Lemma D.9.51

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 114)
)
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{\mu X.P}}\ M
then
\mathrm{unfold}{}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P[\mu X.P/X]}}\ \mathtt{unfold}\ M.

Lemma D.9.52

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 115)
)
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P[\mu X.P/X]}}\ M
then
\mathrm{fold}{}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{\mu X.P}}\ \mathtt{fold}\ M.

Lemma D.9.53

(
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 116)
)
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P\to R}}\ M
and
n\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{P}}\ N,
then
m[n]\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{R}}\ MN.

It is also helpful to state some closure lemmas for the quantum logical relations. However, instead of stating this for the logical relations on configurations, it is more convenient to extend those relations to
quantum terms
and establish the closure properties for them.

Notation D.9.54.

Given a quantum term
\Phi;\mathbf{x}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}:\mathbf{A}_{n}\vdash\mathbf{q}:\mathbf{B}
and a value configuration
\Phi\vdash\mathcal{V}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{n};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}

where
\mathcal{V}=[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{n}]
, we shall write
[\mathbf{q}\twoheadleftarrow\mathcal{V}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{q}[\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{n}/\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}]]
for the configuration obtained by performing the indicated substitution.
Then
\Phi\vdash[\mathbf{q}\twoheadleftarrow\mathcal{V}]:\mathbf{B};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}.

Definition D.9.55.

For each closed quantum type
\mathbf{B}
and quantum context
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{x}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}:\mathbf{A}_{n}
, we define a
logical relation

\displaystyle\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{B}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbf{Q}({\llbracket\mathbf{\Gamma}\rrbracket},{\llbracket\mathbf{B}\rrbracket})\times\{\mathbf{q}\ |\ \cdot;\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{q}:\mathbf{B}\}\qquad\text{by}

\displaystyle q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{B}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\text{ iff }\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\forall\left(\cdot\vdash\mathcal{V}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{n};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}\right).(q\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}})\circ{\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft^{k}_{\mathbf{B}}[\mathbf{q}\twoheadleftarrow\mathcal{V}],

where the second quantifier ranges over well-formed
value confiugrations
of the indicated type.

Lemma D.9.56
.

For any quantum value
\cdot;\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{B}
, we have that
{\llbracket\mathbf{v}\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{v}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{x}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}:\mathbf{A}_{n}
and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{V}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{n};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
be arbitrary.
By the Substitution Lemma
7.4.7
, it follows that
{\llbracket[\mathbf{v}\twoheadleftarrow\mathcal{V}]\rrbracket}=({\llbracket\mathbf{v}\rrbracket}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}})\circ{\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}.

Then the proof follows by Lemma
D.3.8
.
∎

Before we may prove the necessary lemmas for terms dealing with observable primitives, the following lemma is useful.

Lemma D.9.57
.

Let
\cdot\vdash v:O
be an
observable
value. Then
{\llbracket v\rrbracket}\vartriangleleft_{O}v.
Furthermore, if
f\vartriangleleft_{O}v
, then
f={\llbracket v\rrbracket}.

Proof.

Observable values have a very simple structure, because they do not involve any use of function space. The lemma follows by straightforward induction on the derivation of
v
using Theorem
D.8.44
.
∎

Lemma D.9.58
.

Let
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
be a configuration and let
F
be some finite index set. Assume that we are given morphisms
c_{i}\in\mathbf{Q}(\mathbb{C},{\llbracket\mathbf{A}\rrbracket}\otimes{\llbracket\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}\rrbracket})
and configurations
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}_{i}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
such that
c_{i}{\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}}\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i\in F
.
Assume further that for each
i\in F
, we are given a reduction path
\pi_{i}\in\mathrm{Paths}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{i})
, such that all paths
\pi_{i}
are distinct.
Then

\sum_{i\in F}P(\pi_{i})c_{i}{\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}}\mathcal{C}.

Proof.

Fully analogous to Lemma
D.9.45
.
∎

Lemma D.9.59
.

If
q_{1}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\mathbf{B}_{1}}\mathbf{q}_{1}
and
q_{2}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{B}_{2}}\mathbf{q}_{2}
, then
q_{1}\otimes q_{2}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{B}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{2}}\mathbf{q}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{q}_{2}
.

Lemma D.9.60
.

If
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{2}}\mathbf{q}
and
r\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2},\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{y}:\mathbf{A}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{r}
, then

r\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes q)\circ\mathrm{swap}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{let\ x\otimes y=q\ in\ r}.

Lemma D.9.61
.

For
i\in\{1,2\},
if
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{q}
then
\mathbf{in}_{i}\circ q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{A}\oplus\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{in}_{i}\ \mathbf{q}
.

Lemma D.9.62
.

If
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\mathbf{A}_{1}\oplus\mathbf{A}_{2}}\mathbf{q}
and
r_{1}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2},\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{r}_{1}
and

r_{2}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2},\mathbf{y}:\mathbf{A}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{r}_{2}
, then

[r_{1},r_{2}]\circ d\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes q)\circ\mathrm{swap}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{(case\ q\ of\ in_{1}x\Rightarrow r_{1}\ |\ in_{2}y\Rightarrow r_{2})}.

Lemma D.9.63
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{Q(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})}}\ M
and
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{q},
then
\beta(m(*))\circ q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{B}}M\mathbf{q}
.

Lemma D.9.64
.

If
c\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{O}}^{k}\mathcal{C}
, then
(*\mapsto r_{\mathbf{O}}(\mathrm{drop}_{k}\circ c))\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{|\mathbf{O}|}}\ \text{run}\ \mathcal{C}.

Lemma D.9.65
.

If
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{|\mathbf{O}|}}\ M
, then
r^{-1}_{\mathbf{O}}(m_{*})\blacktriangleleft_{\cdot\vdash\mathbf{O}}\textbf{init}\ M.

Lemma D.9.66
.

Let
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\mathbf{O}}\mathbf{q}
. Let
x:|\mathbf{O}|;\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{r}:\mathbf{A}
be a term and
r:{\llbracket|\mathbf{O}|\rrbracket}\to\mathbf{Q}({\llbracket\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\rrbracket},{\llbracket\mathbf{A}\rrbracket})
a Scott-continuous function such that for any observable value
\cdot\vdash V:|\mathbf{O}|
we have that

r_{{\llbracket V\rrbracket}}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{r}[V/x].
Then
\widehat{r}_{*}\circ(q\otimes\mathrm{id})\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{let}\ x=\text{lift}\ \mathbf{q}\ \mathbf{in\ r}.

Lemma D.9.67
.

If
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{P[\mu X.P/X]}}\mathbf{q}
then
\mathrm{fold}\circ q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{\mu X.P}}\mathbf{fold}\ \mathbf{q}
.

Lemma D.9.68
.

If
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{\mu X.P}}\mathbf{q}
then
\mathrm{unfold}\circ q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{P[\mu X.P/X]}}\mathbf{unfold}\ \mathbf{q}
.

Lemma D.9.69
.

If
q\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\vdash\mathbf{I}}\mathbf{q}
and
r\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{r}
then
\cong\circ(q\otimes r)\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{2}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r}
.

D.10.
Fundamental Lemma and Strong Adequacy

We extend the definition of the logical relations to cover
all terms
, including those whose non-linear context may be non-empty.

Definition D.10.70.

For any classical term
x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}\vdash M:R,
and morphism
m\in\mathbf{DCPO}_{\mathcal{M}}({\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket}\times\cdots\times{\llbracket P_{n}\rrbracket},{\llbracket R\rrbracket})
we shall write
m\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}\vdash R}}\ M

iff
\forall(v_{1}\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}}V_{1}),\ldots,(v_{n}\vartriangleleft_{P_{n}}V_{n})
we have that
m\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\ \vec{v}\ \mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{R}}\ M[\vec{V}/\vec{x}].

Definition D.10.71.

For any quantum term
x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n};\mathbf{y}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{m}:\mathbf{A}_{m}\vdash\mathbf{q}:\mathbf{B}
and morphism
q\in\mathbf{DCPO}({\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket}\times\cdots\times{\llbracket P_{n}\rrbracket},\mathbf{Q}({\llbracket\mathbf{A}_{1}\rrbracket}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\llbracket\mathbf{A}_{m}\rrbracket},{\llbracket\mathbf{B}\rrbracket}))

we shall write
q\blacktriangleleft_{x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n};\mathbf{y}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{m}:\mathbf{A}_{m}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{q}

iff
\forall(v_{1}\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}}V_{1}),\ldots,(v_{n}\vartriangleleft_{P_{n}}V_{n})
we have that
q_{\vec{v}}{\blacktriangleleft}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{m}:\mathbf{A}_{m}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{q}[\vec{V}/\vec{x}].

We may now prove the Fundamental Lemma which then easily implies our adequacy result.

Lemma D.10.72

(Fundamental)
.

For any classical term
\Phi\vdash M:R
and any quantum term
\Phi;\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{q}:\mathbf{B}
we have that

{\llbracket M\rrbracket}\ \overline{\vartriangleleft_{\Phi\vdash R}}\ M\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\llbracket\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}{\blacktriangleleft}_{\Phi;\mathbf{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{q}

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the term.

Classical Lambda Abstractions.

The case for classical lambda abstractions follows using exactly the same arguments as
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Lemma 117)
.

Quantum Lambda Abstractions.

The case for quantum lambda abstractions follows using similar arguments which we now present.

Let us assume that the term of the induction hypothesis is

x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n};\mathbf{y}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{m}:\mathbf{A}_{m}\vdash\mathbf{q}:\mathbf{B}.

Let
(v_{1}\vartriangleleft_{P_{1}}V_{1}),\ldots,(v_{n}\vartriangleleft_{P_{n}}V_{n})
be arbitrary.
Let us write
l\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}{\llbracket{\lambda}\vec{\mathbf{y}}.\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\ \vec{v}\ \mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
and
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}{\lambda}\vec{\mathbf{y}}.\mathbf{q}[\vec{V}/\vec{x}]
.
Observe that
l\in\mathbf{TD}
and therefore by Theorem
D.8.44
(C5), we may equivalently show that

l\vartriangleleft_{Q(\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{m},\mathbf{B})}R.

By Theorem
D.8.44
(A5), this is in turn equivalent to showing that

l\leq{\llbracket R\rrbracket}\text{ and }\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\forall(\cdot\vdash[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}]:\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{m};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}).

(\beta(l(*))\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{\llbracket[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}]\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{B}}^{k}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,R(\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m})]

The inequality is satisfied, because

\displaystyle l

\displaystyle={\llbracket{\lambda}\vec{\mathbf{y}}.\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\ \vec{v}\ \mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle\leq{\llbracket{\lambda}\vec{\mathbf{y}}.\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.9pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\varodot$}}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\ \vec{{\llbracket V\rrbracket}}\ \mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle(\text{Theorem \ref{thm:formal-relations} (C1)})

\displaystyle={\llbracket R\rrbracket}.

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{lem:term-substitution}})

For the other requirement, we reason as follows

\displaystyle\beta(l(*))

\displaystyle=\beta({\llbracket{\lambda}\vec{\mathbf{y}}.\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}_{\vec{v}})

\displaystyle(\text{Definition})

\displaystyle=\beta(\mathcal{J}{\llbracket\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}_{\vec{v}})

\displaystyle={\llbracket\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}_{\vec{v}}

\displaystyle{\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{y}_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{m}:\mathbf{A}_{m}\vdash\mathbf{B}}}\mathbf{q}[\vec{V}/\vec{x}].

(Induction Hypothesis)

It now follows (by definition) that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\cdot\vdash[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}]:\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{m};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
we have that

(\beta(l(*))\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{\llbracket[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}]\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{B}}^{k}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{q}[\vec{\mathbf{v}}/\vec{\mathbf{y}},\vec{V}/\vec{x}]].

Finally, observe that
[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,R(\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m})]\xrightarrow{1}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{q}[\vec{\mathbf{v}}/\vec{\mathbf{y}},\vec{V}/\vec{x}]]
and then by Lemma
D.9.58
it follows that

(\beta(l(*))\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{\llbracket[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}]\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{B}}^{k}[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,R(\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m})],

as required.

The cases for terms whose formation rules do not have a premise follow by straightforward verification.
All other cases follow by induction using the relevant closure lemma from Section
D.9
.
∎

Corollary D.10.73
.

For any closed quantum configuration
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
, we have that
{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}\mathcal{C}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{q}]
. It follows that for
n=\dim(\lvert\psi\rangle)-k
we have
\mathbf{x}_{1}:\textnormal{{qbit}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}:\textnormal{{qbit}}\vdash\mathbf{q}:\mathbf{A}
and then by the Fundamental Lemma we know that

{\llbracket\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{x}_{1}:\textnormal{{qbit}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}:\textnormal{{qbit}}\vdash\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{q}.

By definition it now follows that for
\cdot\vdash[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{x}_{n}]:\textnormal{{qbit}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\textnormal{{qbit}};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
we have

({\llbracket\mathbf{q}\rrbracket}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}})\circ{\llbracket[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{x}_{n}]\rrbracket}\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}[\mathbf{q}\twoheadleftarrow[\lvert\psi\rangle,\ell,\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{x}_{n}]].

The LHS is precisely
{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}
and the RHS is exactly
\mathcal{C}
which completes the proof.
∎

Adequacy now follows as a consequence of the Fundamental Lemma.

Theorem D.10.74

(Strong Adequacy)
.

Let
\cdot\vdash m:P
be a closed classical term and
\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}:\mathbf{A};\textnormal{{qbit}}^{k}
a closed quantum configuration. Then:

\displaystyle{\llbracket m\rrbracket}=\sum_{v\in\text{Val}(m)}P(m\xrightarrow{}_{*}v){\llbracket v\rrbracket}\qquad\qquad{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}=\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{ValC}(\mathcal{C})}P(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}_{*}\mathcal{V}){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}.

Proof.

The equation for classical terms may be established using exactly the same arguments as
(
Jia
et al. 2021
, Theorem 118)
. The equation for quantum configurations also follows using the same arguments which we repeat now.
Let

u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{ValC}(\mathcal{C})}P(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}_{*}\mathcal{V}){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}.

From Corollary
D.0.1
, we know that
{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}\geq u.
It remains to show the converse inequality.
Towards this end, observe that
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathop{\downarrow}u
.
To establish this, we reason as follows. Taking an arbitrary element of
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C})
as in Definition
D.3.7
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in F}P(\pi){\llbracket\mathcal{V}_{\pi}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\cup\{\mathcal{V}_{\pi}|\pi\in F\}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in F\\
\mathcal{V}_{\pi}=\mathcal{V}\end{subarray}}P(\pi)\right){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\cup\{\mathcal{V}_{\pi}|\pi\in F\}}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{Paths}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{V})}P(\pi)\right){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\cup\{\mathcal{V}_{\pi}|\pi\in F\}}P(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}_{*}\mathcal{V}){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{ValC}(\mathcal{C})}P(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}_{*}\mathcal{V}){\llbracket\mathcal{V}\rrbracket}.

The set
\mathop{\downarrow}u
is Scott-closed and therefore
\mathcal{S}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathop{\downarrow}u
. By Corollary
D.10.73
, we know
that
{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}{\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}}\mathcal{C}.
By definition of
{\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k}}
it follows
{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}\in\mathcal{S}(\blacktriangleleft_{\mathbf{A}}^{k};\mathcal{C})
and therefore
{\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}\leq u,
thus finishing the proof.
∎
